}a_{1}\geq\epsilon^{(i)},\\
0\mbox{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Remark 4.1
.

We define a pairing between
D_{E}^{\mathrm{s}}\otimes D_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}
and itself as follows:

\displaystyle\forall a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1},

\displaystyle\langle a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime},a\otimes b\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}.

It is possible to transpose
\partial^{(j)}
: define

d^{(j)}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}D_{E}^{\mathrm{s}}\otimes D_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}&\longrightarrow&D_{E}^{\mathrm{s}}\otimes D_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}\\
a\otimes b&\longmapsto&(b_{j}+1)\left(a+\epsilon^{(j)}\right)\otimes\left(b+\epsilon^{(j)}\right).\end{array}\right.

Then, for any
x,x^{\prime}\in D_{E}^{\mathrm{s}}\otimes D_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}
,

\langle\partial^{(j)}(x^{\prime}),x\rangle=\langle x,d^{(j)}(x)\rangle.

Note that
d^{(j)}
is not locally nilpotent. It is not possible to transpose
\phi^{\lambda}
: such a transpose should satisfy

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1},

\displaystyle\left(\phi^{\lambda}\right)^{*}(a\otimes b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}}\lambda^{l}\binom{b+l}{l}(a+l)\otimes(b+l),

which does not belong to
D_{E}^{\mathrm{s}}\otimes D_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}
but rather to a completion of this vector space. The associated Butcher-Connes-Kreimer would require infinite sums of tensors. Such objects are considered in
[
5
]
.

4.2
An example for stochastic PDEs, with noise

In order to treat noises, we augment the spaces of decorations
D_{E}^{\mathrm{s}}
and
D_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}
by adding two symbols:

\displaystyle\overline{D_{E}^{\mathrm{s}}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Vect}(\mathbb{N}^{d+1}\sqcup\{\Xi\}),

\displaystyle\overline{D_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Vect}(\mathbb{N}^{d+1}\sqcup\{\star\}).

We extend
\phi^{\lambda}
as follows:

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1},

\displaystyle\overline{\phi}^{\lambda}(a\otimes\star)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\overline{\phi}^{\lambda}(\Xi\otimes b)

\displaystyle=\Xi\otimes b,

\displaystyle\overline{\phi}^{\lambda}(\Xi\otimes\star)

\displaystyle=0.

Proposition 4.8
.

This extension
\overline{\phi}^{\lambda}
of
\phi^{\lambda}
is tree-compatible.

Proof.

We put
D^{\prime}_{E}=\mathrm{Vect}(\Xi)
and
D^{\prime}_{V}=\mathrm{Vect}(\star)
, and we equipped
D^{\prime}_{E}\otimes D^{\prime}_{V}
with the zero map
\phi^{\prime}
, which is obviously tree-compatible.
Then, with the notations of Proposition
2.1
,
\overline{\phi}^{\lambda}=\phi^{\lambda}\oplus_{0,1}\phi^{\prime}
.
∎

Proposition 4.9
.

For any
i\in\llbracket 0;d\rrbracket
, we extend
\psi_{V}(X_{i})
to
\overline{D_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}}
and
\psi_{E}(X_{i})
to
\overline{D_{E}^{\mathrm{s}}}
by

\displaystyle\overline{\psi}_{V}(X_{i})(\star)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\overline{\psi}_{E}(X_{i})(\Xi)

\displaystyle=0.

The pair
(\overline{\psi}_{E},\overline{\psi}_{V})
is
\overline{\phi}^{\lambda}
-compatible, in the sense of Corollary
3.10
.

Proof.

The first two conditions are obvious. Let us prove the last one. Let us fix
i\in\llbracket 0;d\rrbracket
. It is enough to prove that

\overline{\phi^{\lambda}}\circ(\overline{\psi}_{E}(X_{i})\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\overline{D_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}}})(x\otimes y)=\overline{\phi^{\lambda}}\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{\overline{D_{E}^{\mathrm{s}}}}\otimes\overline{\psi}_{V}(X_{i}))(x\otimes y)-(\mathrm{Id}_{\overline{D_{E}^{\mathrm{s}}}}\otimes\overline{\psi}_{V}(X_{i}))\circ\overline{\phi^{\lambda}}(x\otimes y)

in the following cases:

•

y=\star
. Then every term is zero.

•

x=\Xi
and
y\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
. Then every term is zero. ∎

Example 4.3
.

If
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
and
i\in\llbracket 0;d\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle X_{i}\triangleleft\hskip-14.22636pt\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern Remark 4.2
.

Let
x=T\otimes W_{T}
and
x^{\prime}=T^{\prime}\otimes W_{T^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{T}(\overline{D_{E}^{\mathrm{s}}},\overline{D_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}})
be two rooted trees such that any vertex is decorated by an element of
\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
or by
\star
, and any edge is decorated by an element of
\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
or by
\Xi
. As
\overline{\phi^{\lambda}}(x\otimes\star)=0
for any
x\in\overline{D_{E}^{\mathrm{s}}}
,
we can rewrite the definition of the products in this way: for any
a\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
,

\displaystyle x\triangleleft^{\overline{\phi^{\lambda}}}_{a}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in V(T^{\prime}),\\
b_{v}\neq\star\end{subarray}}T\curvearrowright_{v}T^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\phi^{\lambda}}_{0,v}\left(a\otimes W_{T}\otimes W_{T^{\prime}}\right).

For example, if
a,a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4}\in\mathrm{Vect}\left(\mathbb{N}^{d+1}\right)
,

Proposition 4.10
.

Let us denote by
\mathfrak{g}
the pre-Lie subalgebra of
\overline{D_{E}^{\mathrm{s}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}(\overline{D_{E}^{\mathrm{s}}},\overline{D_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}})
generated by the elements

\displaystyle\Xi\otimes\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}}\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.3264pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\star}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}}\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.3264pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\star}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-2.6389pt\raise-15.6111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Xi}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}}\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise-31.2222pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.3264pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\star}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array},

\displaystyle a\otimes\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}}\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.3264pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\star}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}}\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.3264pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\star}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-2.16882pt\raise-15.6111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{a}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}}\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise-31.2222pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.3264pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\star}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array},

\displaystyle a\otimes\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.14583pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}}\hbox{\kern-5.14583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.47223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{b}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}}\hbox{\kern-5.14583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.47223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{b}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-2.16882pt\raise-15.6111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{a}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}}\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise-31.2222pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.3264pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\star}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array},

where
a,b\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
.
As a vector space, it is generated by the set of tensors
a\otimes T\otimes W_{T}
, where
a\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}\sqcup\{\Xi\}
and
T\otimes W_{T}
is a rooted whose vertices are decorated by elements of
\mathbb{N}^{d+1}\sqcup\{\star\}
and edges by elements of
\mathbb{N}^{d+1}\sqcup\{\Xi\}
,
with the following conditions:

•

If
a=\Xi
, then
T=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}}\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.3264pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\star}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
.

•

If
e\in E(T)
is decorated by
\Xi
, then
d_{t(e)}=\star
.

•

If
v\in V(T)
is decorated by
\star
, then it is a leaf of
T
.

These elements will be called
\Xi
-admissible.

Proof.

Let us observe firstly that the pre-Lie product is given by

\displaystyle a\otimes x\triangleleft a^{\prime}\otimes x^{\prime}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in V(T^{\prime}),\\
b_{v}\neq\star\end{subarray}}a^{\prime}\otimes T\curvearrowright_{v}T^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\phi^{\lambda}}_{0,v}\left(a\otimes W_{T}\otimes W_{T^{\prime}}\right).

As
\overline{\phi^{\lambda}}(\Xi\otimes b)=\Xi\otimes b
for any
b\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
, we obtain that if
a\otimes x
and
a^{\prime}\otimes x^{\prime}
are
\Xi
-admissible, then any term in
a\otimes x\triangleleft a^{\prime}\otimes x^{\prime}
is also
\Xi
-admissible:
therefore, these conditions define a pre-Lie subalgebra. As it contains the generators of
\mathfrak{g}
, it contains
\mathfrak{g}
.

Let
a\otimes x
be
\Xi
-admissible, and let us show that it belongs to
\mathfrak{g}
. If
a=\Xi
, then
a\otimes x=\Xi\otimes\star\in\mathfrak{g}
. Let us assume that
a\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
. We proceed by the number
n
of vertices of the underlying tree.
If
n=1
, then
a\otimes x
is a generator of
\mathfrak{g}
. Let us put
T=B^{+}(T_{1}\ldots T_{k})
and let us proceed by induction on
k
.
If
k=0
, then
a\otimes x
is a generator of
\mathfrak{g}
. Otherwise, let us put
x^{\prime}
the decorated rooted tree corresponding to
T_{1}
and
x^{\prime\prime}
the rooted tree corresponding to
B^{+}(T_{2}\ldots T_{k})
.
If
c
is the decoration of the root of
T
(note that
b\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
), and
b
the decoration of the edge between the root of
T
and the root of
T_{1}
, let us consider

\left(\phi^{\lambda}\right)^{-1}(b\otimes c)=\phi^{-\lambda}(b\otimes c)=\sum_{i}\mu_{i}b_{i}\otimes c_{i}.

Let us denote by
x^{\prime\prime}_{i}
the element obtained from
x^{\prime\prime}
be changing the decoration of the root into
c_{i}
, instead of
c
. Then

\sum_{i}\mu_{i}b_{i}\otimes x^{\prime}\triangleleft a\otimes x^{\prime\prime}_{i}=a\otimes x+\mbox{a sum of terms of $\Xi$-admissible trees with $n$ vertices and $k^{\prime}=k-1$}.

By the induction hypothesis on
n
,
b_{i}\otimes x^{\prime}
and
a\otimes x^{\prime\prime}_{i}
belong to
\mathfrak{g}
. By the induction hypothesis on
k
, we deduce that
a\otimes x\in\mathfrak{g}
.
∎

4.3
Examples in small dimensions

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
f
be an endomorphism of
D_{E}
and
g
be an endomorphism of
D_{V}
. Then
f\otimes g
is tree-compatible.

Proof.

Indeed, if
\phi=f\otimes g
,

\displaystyle\phi_{13}

\displaystyle=f\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{D_{E}}\otimes g,

\displaystyle\phi_{23}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Id}_{D_{E}}\otimes f\otimes g.

So
\phi_{13}
and
\phi_{23}
commute.
∎

Corollary 4.12
.

Let us assume that
D_{V}
or
D_{E}
is 1-dimensional. Then any endomorphism of
D_{E}\otimes D_{V}
is tree-compatible.

Proof.

If
D_{E}
is one-dimensional, then any endomorphism of
D_{E}\otimes D_{V}
is of the form
f\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{D_{V}}
, where
f
is an endomorphism of
D_{E}
. The proof is similar if
D_{V}
is one-dimensional.
∎

When
D_{E}
and
D_{V}
are finite-dimensional, we can work with matrices:

Proposition 4.13
.

Let us assume that
D_{E}
and
D_{V}
are finite-dimensional, with respective dimensions
m
et
n
, and bases
\mathcal{B}_{E}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})
and
\mathcal{B}_{V}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})
.
The basis
\mathcal{B}_{E}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{V}
of
D_{E}\otimes D_{V}
is

\mathcal{B}_{E}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{V}=(a_{1}\otimes b_{1},\ldots,a_{1}\otimes b_{n},\ldots,a_{m}\otimes b_{1},\ldots,a_{m}\otimes b_{n}).

Let
\phi
be an endomorphism of
D_{E}\otimes D_{V}
. Its matrix in the basis
\mathcal{B}_{E}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{V}
is written under the form

\begin{pmatrix}A_{11}&\ldots&A_{1m}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
A_{m1}&\ldots&A_{mm}\end{pmatrix},

with for any
i,j\in\llbracket 1;m\rrbracket
,
A_{i,j}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{K})
. Then:

1.

\phi
is tree-admissible if, and only if, for any
i,j,k,l\in\llbracket 1;m\rrbracket
,
A_{ij}A_{kl}=A_{kl}A_{ij}
.

2.

\phi
is the tensor product of an endomorphism of
D_{E}
and an endomorphism of
D_{V}
if, and only if,

\mathrm{Rank}(A_{11},\ldots,A_{mn})\leq 1.

Proof.

1. In order to simplify the writing, we shall identify any
b\in D_{V}
with its coordinates vector in
\mathbb{K}^{n}
. Let
j\in\llbracket 1;m\rrbracket
and
b\in D_{V}
. Then

\phi(a_{j}\otimes b)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\otimes A_{ij}b,

so, for any
i,j\in\llbracket 1;m\rrbracket
and
b\in D_{V}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{23}\circ\phi_{13}(a_{i}\otimes a_{j}\otimes b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l=1}^{m}a_{k}\otimes a_{l}\otimes A_{lj}A_{ki}b,

\displaystyle\phi_{13}\circ\phi_{23}(a_{i}\otimes a_{j}\otimes b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l=1}^{m}a_{k}\otimes a_{l}\otimes A_{ki}A_{lj}b.

Therefore,

\phi
is tree-compatible

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\forall i,j\in\llbracket 1;m\rrbracket,\>\forall b\in D_{V},\>\sum_{k,l=1}^{m}a_{k}\otimes a_{l}\otimes A_{lj}A_{ki}b=\sum_{k,l=1}^{m}a_{k}\otimes a_{l}\otimes A_{ki}A_{lj}b

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\forall i,j,k,l\in\llbracket 1;m\rrbracket,\>\forall b\in D_{V},\>A_{lj}A_{ki}b=A_{ki}A_{lj}b

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\forall i,j,k,l\in\llbracket 1;m\rrbracket,\>A_{lj}A_{ki}=A_{ki}A_{lj}.

2.
\Longrightarrow
. Let us assume that
\phi=\phi_{E}\otimes\phi_{V}
. Let us denote by
B=(b_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq m}
the matrix of
\phi_{E}
in the basis
\mathcal{B}_{E}
and by
A
the matrix of
\phi_{V}
in the basis
\mathcal{B}_{V}
.
Then, for any
i,j\in\llbracket 1;m\rrbracket
,
A_{ij}=b_{ij}A
, so
\mathrm{Rank}(A_{11},\ldots,A_{mm})\leq 1
.

\Longleftarrow
. If so, there exist matrices
A\in M_{n}(\mathbb{K})
and
B=(b_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq m}\in M_{m}(\mathbb{K})
such that for any
i,j\in\llbracket 1;m\rrbracket
,
A_{ij}=b_{ij}A
.
Let
\phi_{E}
be the endomorphism of
D_{E}
whose matrix in the basis
D_{E}
is
B
and
\phi_{V}
the endomorphism of
D_{V}
whose matrix in the basis
D_{V}
is
A
. Then the matrix of
\phi_{E}\otimes\phi_{V}
in the basis
\mathcal{B}_{E}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{V}
is

\begin{pmatrix}b_{11}A&\ldots&b_{1m}A\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
b_{m1}A&\ldots&b_{mm}A\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{11}&\ldots&A_{1m}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
A_{m1}&\ldots&A_{mm}\end{pmatrix},

so
\phi=\phi_{E}\otimes\phi_{V}
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

The matrix of
\phi^{*}
in the dual basis
(a_{1}^{*}\otimes b_{1}^{*},\ldots,a_{m}^{*}\otimes b_{n}^{*})
is

\begin{pmatrix}A_{11}&\ldots&A_{1,m}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
A_{m1}&\ldots&A_{mn}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{11}^{\top}&\ldots&A_{m1}^{\top}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
A_{1m}^{\top}&\ldots&A_{mm}^{\top}\end{pmatrix}.

Notations 4.3
.

For any
(a,b)\in\mathbb{K}^{2}
, we consider the
2\times 2
matrices

\displaystyle J(a,b)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&a\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle D(a,b)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&b\end{pmatrix}.

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
D_{E}
be finite-dimensional
\mathbb{C}
-vector space of dimension
m
and
D_{V}
be a 2-dimensional
\mathbb{C}
-vector space, and let
\phi
be an endomorphism of
D_{E}\otimes D_{V}
.
Then
\phi
is tree-admissible if, and only if, there exists a basis
\mathcal{B}_{E}
of
D_{E}
and
\mathcal{B}_{V}
of
D_{V}
such that the matrix of
\phi
in the basis
\mathcal{B}_{E}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{V}
has one of the following forms:

\displaystyle J(A,B)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}J(a_{11},b_{11})&\ldots&J(a_{1m},b_{1m})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
J(a_{m1},b_{m1})&\ldots&J(a_{mm},b_{mm})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\mbox{or }D(A,B)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}D(a_{11},b_{11})&\ldots&D(a_{1m},b_{1m})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
D(a_{m1},b_{m1})&\ldots&D(a_{mm},b_{mm})\end{pmatrix},

(14)

with
A=(a_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq m}
and
B=(b_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq m}
in
M_{m}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

For any
A\in M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, we put

\mathcal{C}(A)=\{B\in M_{2}(\mathbb{C})\mid AB=BA\}.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{K}
. We leave as an exercise to prove that

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(D(a,b))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\{D(c,d)\mid(c,d)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\}\mbox{ if }a\neq b,\\
M_{2}(\mathbb{C})\mbox{ if }a=b.\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(J(a,b))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\{J(c,d)\mid(c,d)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\}\mbox{ if }b\neq 0,\\
M_{2}(\mathbb{C})\mbox{ if }b=0.\end{cases}

Therefore, Proposition
4.13
implies that if the matrix of
\phi
in a basis
\mathcal{B}_{E}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{V}
has one of the form of (
14
), then
\phi
is tree-compatible.

Let us now assume that
\phi
is tree-compatible. Let us choose arbitrary bases
\mathcal{B}_{E}
and
\mathcal{B}_{V}
of, respectively,
D_{E}
and
D_{V}
. The matrix of
\phi
in
\mathcal{B}_{E}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{V}
is denoted by

M=\begin{pmatrix}M_{11}&\ldots&M_{m1}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
M_{m1}&\ldots&M_{mm}\end{pmatrix},

with for any
i,j\in\llbracket 1;m\rrbracket
,
M_{ij}\in M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. If all these
2\times 2
matrices are multiples of the identity matrix, then

M=\begin{pmatrix}D(a_{11},a_{11})&\ldots&D(a_{1m},a_{1m})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
D(a_{m1},a_{m1})&\ldots&D(a_{mm},a_{mm})\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}J(a_{11},0)&\ldots&J(a_{1m},0)\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
J(a_{m1},0)&\ldots&J(a_{mm},0)\end{pmatrix},

with
(a_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq m}\in M_{m}(\mathbb{C})
. Otherwise, let us fix
i_{0},j_{0}\in\llbracket 1;m\rrbracket
such that
M_{i_{0}j_{0}}
is not a multiple of the identity matrix. As we work over
\mathbb{C}
, there exists a matrix
P\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
such that

P^{-1}M_{i_{0}j_{0}}P=J(a_{i_{0}j_{0}},b_{i_{0}j_{0}})
with
b_{i_{0}j_{0}}\neq 0
or
D(a_{i_{0}j_{0}},b_{i_{0}j_{0}})
with
a_{i_{0}j_{0}}\neq b_{i_{0}j_{0}}
. Changing the basis of
D_{V}
according to
P
,
M
is replaced by

\begin{pmatrix}P^{-1}M_{11}P&\ldots&P^{-1}M_{m1}P\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
P^{-1}M_{m1}P&\ldots&P^{-1}M_{mm}P\end{pmatrix}.

Consequently, we can assume that
M_{i_{0}j_{0}}=J(a_{i_{0}j_{0}},b_{i_{0}j_{0}})
or
D(a_{i_{0}j_{0}},b_{i_{0}j_{0}})
. As
\phi
is tree-compatible, for any
i,j\in\llbracket 1;m\rrbracket
,
M_{ij}\in\mathcal{C}(M_{i_{0}j_{0}})
.
If
M_{i_{0}j_{0}}=J(a_{i_{0}j_{0}},b_{i_{0}j_{0}})
, then for any
i,j\in\llbracket 1;m\rrbracket
,
M_{ij}
is of the form
J(a_{ij},b_{ij})
, as
b_{i_{0}j_{0}}\neq 0
;
if
M_{i_{0}j_{0}}=D(a_{i_{0}j_{0}},b_{i_{0}j_{0}})
, then for any
i,j\in\llbracket 1;m\rrbracket
,
M_{ij}
is of the form
D(a_{ij},b_{ij})
, as
a_{i_{0}j_{0}}\neq b_{i_{0}j_{0}}
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Of course, this form is not unique. It is not difficult to prove that:

1.

If
\phi
has a matrix of the form
D(A,B)
, then the other possible forms for matrices of
\phi
are
D(P^{-1}AP,P^{-1}BP)
and
D(P^{-1}BP,P^{-1}AP)
, with
P\in\mathrm{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{C})
.

2.

If
\phi
has a matrix of the form
J(A,B)
, then the other possible forms for matrices of
\phi
are
J(P^{-1}AP,\alpha P^{-1}BP)
, with
P\in\mathrm{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{C})
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

In both cases,
P
corresponds to a change of basis for
D_{E}
; in the first case, the permutation of
A
and
B
corresponds to a change of basis of
D_{V}
; in the second case, the scalar
\alpha
corresponds to a change of basis of
D_{V}
.

Remark 4.5
.

If the matrix of
\phi
in a convenient basis is
J(A,B)
, respectively
D(A,B)
, then in a convenient basis, the matrix of
\phi^{*}
is
J(A^{\top},B^{\top})
, respectively
D(A^{\top},B^{\top})
.

We end this section by an observation of the invertibility of
J(A,B)
and
D(A,B)
:

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
A,B\in M_{m}(\mathbb{K})
. Then:

1.

D(A,B)
is invertible if, and only if,
A
and
B
are invertible.

2.

J(A,B)
is invertible if, and only if,
A
is invertible.

Proof.

As the blocks
M_{ij}
of
M=D(A,B)
or
J(A,B)
pairwise commute, we can apply the result on determinants by blocks
[
3
]
and we obtain that

\displaystyle\det(M)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{j=1}^{m}M_{\sigma(j)j}\right).

If
M=D(A,B)
, then
M_{ij}=D(a_{ij},b_{ij})
and

\det(M)=\det\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{j=1}^{m}a_{\sigma(j)j}&0\\
0&\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{j=1}^{m}b_{\sigma(j)j}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\det(A)&0\\
0&\det(B)\end{pmatrix}=\det(A)\det(B).

If
M=J(A,B)
, then
M_{ij}=J(a_{ij},b_{ij})
and

\det(M)=\det\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{j=1}^{m}a_{\sigma(j)j}&?\\
0&\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{j=1}^{m}a_{\sigma(j)j}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\det(A)&?\\
0&\det(A)\end{pmatrix}=\det(A)^{2}.\qed

5
Appendix : freeness of
D_{E}\otimes\mathcal{T}(D_{E},D_{V})

We here prove that the pre-Lie algebra
(D_{E}\otimes\mathcal{T}(D_{E},D_{V}))
(without deformation by a tree-compatible map
\phi
) is free. We use for this Livernet’s rigidity theorem
[
26
]
.

Proposition 5.1
.

The pre-Lie algebra
(D_{E}\otimes\mathcal{T}(D_{E},D_{V}),\triangleleft)
is freely generated by the elements
a\otimes\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.14583pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}}\hbox{\kern-5.14583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.47223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{b}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
, with
a\in D_{E}
and
b\in D_{V}
.

Proof.

We define a non-associative, permutative coproduct (shortly, NAP) coproduct
\rho
by the following:
if
a\in D_{E}
and
x=T\otimes W_{T}\in\mathcal{T}(D_{E},D_{v})
,

\rho(a\otimes x)=\sum_{e\in E(T),\>s(e)=\mathrm{rt}(T)}\left(d_{e}\otimes P^{e}(T)\otimes W_{P^{e}(T)}\right)\otimes\left(a\otimes R^{e}(T)\otimes W_{R_{e}}(T)\right),

where:

•

\mathrm{rt}(T)
is the root of
T
.

•

For any
e\in E(T)
,
P^{e}(T)
is the subtree of
T
formed by the vertices
v\in V(T)
with a path from
t(e)
to
v
, and
R^{e}(T)
is the subtree of
T
formed by the other vertices.

Note that, up to the order of the factors,

W_{T}=d_{E}\otimes W_{P^{e}(T)}\otimes W_{R^{e}(T)},

as
V(T)
is the disjoint union of
V(P^{e}(T))
and
V(R^{e}(T))
and
E(T)
is the disjoint union of
E(P^{e}(T))
and
E(R^{e}(T))
, minus
\{e\}
.
Then

\displaystyle(\mathrm{Id}\otimes\rho)\circ\rho(a\otimes x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\neq e^{\prime}\in E(T),\\
s(e)=s(e^{\prime})=\mathrm{rt}(T)\end{subarray}}(d_{e}\otimes P^{e}(T)\otimes W_{P^{e}(T)})\otimes(d_{e^{\prime}}\otimes P^{e^{\prime}}(T)\otimes W_{P^{e^{\prime}}(T)})\otimes(a\otimes R^{e,e^{\prime}}(T)\otimes W_{R^{e,e^{\prime}}}(T)),

which is invariant by permutation of the first two factors:
\rho
is indeed a NAP coproduct.
A study of the edges of
T\curvearrowright_{v}T^{\prime}
proves that for any
a,a^{\prime}\in D_{E}
, for any
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(D_{E},D_{V})
,

\displaystyle\rho((a\otimes x)\triangleleft(a^{\prime}\otimes x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(a\otimes x)\otimes(a^{\prime}\otimes x^{\prime})+\sum\left((a\otimes x)\triangleleft(a^{\prime}\otimes x^{\prime})^{(1)}\right)\otimes(a^{\prime}\otimes x^{\prime})^{(2)}

\displaystyle+\sum(a^{\prime}\otimes x^{\prime})^{(1)}\otimes\left((a\otimes x)\triangleleft(a^{\prime}\otimes x^{\prime})^{(2)}\right),

with Sweedler’s notation
\rho(a\otimes x)=\sum(a\otimes x)^{(1)}\otimes(a\otimes x)^{(2)}
.
By Livernet’s rigidity theorem
[
26
]
,
D_{E}\otimes\mathcal{T}(D_{E},D_{V})
is freely generated by
\mathrm{Ker}(\rho)
.

It remains to compute
\mathrm{Ker}(\rho)
. We define a new product
\blacktriangleleft
on
D_{E}\otimes\mathcal{T}(D_{E},D_{V})
by

a\otimes T\otimes W_{T}\blacktriangleleft a^{\prime}\otimes T^{\prime}\otimes W_{T^{\prime}}=a^{\prime}\otimes T\curvearrowright_{\mathrm{rt}(T^{\prime})}T^{\prime}\otimes a\otimes W_{T}\otimes W_{T^{\prime}}.

Then, for any
a\otimes T\otimes W_{T}\in D_{E}\otimes\mathcal{T}(D_{E},D_{V})
,

\blacktriangleleft\circ\rho(T\otimes W_{T})=\alpha_{T}T\otimes W_{T},

where
\alpha_{T}
is the number of edges
e\in E(T)
such that
s(e)=\mathrm{rt}(T)
.
Therefore, if
x=\displaystyle\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes T_{i}\otimes W_{T_{i}}\in\mathrm{Ker}(\rho)
, then

\blacktriangleleft\circ\rho(x)=0=\sum_{i}\alpha_{T_{i}}a_{i}\otimes T_{i}.

Therefore,
x
is a sum of terms
a\otimes\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.14583pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}}\hbox{\kern-5.14583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.47223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{b}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
. The converse is immediate. ∎

If
\phi
is bijective, as
\Theta_{\phi}
is an isomorphism from
(\mathcal{T}(D,E,D_{V}),\triangleleft)
to
(\mathcal{T}(D,E,D_{V}),\triangleleft^{\phi})
:

Corollary 5.2
.

If
\phi
is an invertible tree-compatible map, then
(D_{E}\otimes\mathcal{T}(D_{E},D_{V}),\triangleleft^{\phi})
is, as a pre-Lie algebra, freely generated by the elements
a\otimes\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.14583pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}}\hbox{\kern-5.14583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.47223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{b}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
, with
a\in D_{E}
and
b\in D_{V}
.

Remark 5.1
.

If
\phi
is not injective, then this pre-Lie algebra is not freely generated by these elements: indeed, if
\displaystyle\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}
is a nonzero element of
\mathrm{Ker}(\phi)
, then if
a^{\prime}\in D_{E}
and
b^{\prime}\in D_{V}
, both nonzero,
